And then other nights I’ll sort of pick at crackers or cereal, just standing in the kitchen, because the idea of cooking a real meal just for myself feels pointless. It’s not one dramatic thing, exactly, but it’s gone on long enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still technically do some of the things I’ve always liked, but it’s more like I’m completing the task than enjoying it.

Like my garden, for example. I used to really love going out there, checking what was coming up, fussing with the plants, watering slowly after work. Now I’ll stand there with the hose and just sort of… get it done. I don’t feel much from it. Sometimes I look at the weeds or the dry spots and think, “I should deal with that,” and then I just don’t start.

And book club too — I still show up sometimes, partly because I know people expect me to and I don’t want to be rude. But I’m not excited about the book or the conversation the way I used to be. I’ll sit there and listen, maybe make a comment or two, and then I find myself watching the clock and leaving early if I can do it without making it obvious.

So yes, I guess the pleasure part is definitely less. I’m still going through the motions, but most of it feels kind of flat.

It’s been… I’d say since my divorce was finalized. That was about a year and seven months ago now, which feels strange to say out loud because in some ways it feels like it just happened, and in other ways it feels like I’ve been living in this gray sort of version of my life for a long time.

So I’d put the start of it around then — about a year and seven months ago. Since the divorce was finalized, really.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and seven months, have there been any stretches as long as two months when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I’m not sure I’d use the word hopeless exactly… I mean, there have definitely been times where I’ve felt discouraged, or like I can’t quite picture things getting lighter in a concrete way. But “hopeless” feels like a big word to me, and I don’t know if I’m there most of the time.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I have. Maybe not every single day in some obvious way, but it’s been there a lot.

I keep going back over the marriage in my head, like replaying conversations or decisions or just whole stretches of time, and I find myself thinking, “I must have failed at something really basic.” Like being a wife, or being emotionally available enough, or noticing things sooner, or fixing things before they got to that point. And I know—at least intellectually, and I would say this to a client without hesitation—that relationships are complicated and it’s almost never just one person’s fault. I know that.

But emotionally, it doesn’t always land that way. It turns into this sense that I should have been better somehow, or I missed something that a more capable person would have understood. So yes, I guess I have often felt bad about myself in that sense. Not necessarily hating myself outright, but carrying this ongoing feeling that I failed at something important.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… yes, in a way. Not that I have a plan to hurt myself. I don’t. I want to be clear about that. I haven’t been thinking, “I’m going to do this specific thing,” or making arrangements or anything like that.

But there are times — usually when I’m really worn down emotionally — where I catch myself thinking that it would be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And even saying that out loud is hard, because part of me immediately wants to minimize it or explain it away, but… it scares me. I know if a client said that to me, I would take it seriously. I wouldn’t just brush past it.

So I guess the honest answer is: I don’t want to harm myself in an active way, but I do sometimes have those thoughts about not having to keep waking up into all of this. And that feels frightening to admit.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because the days kind of blur together when I think back on it. There have definitely been stretches where I felt low a lot of the time, but whether it was most of the day, nearly every day, for a full two weeks… I don’t know. I don’t want to overstate it.

Since the divorce, it’s been more like this heaviness that comes and goes in waves. Some days I can function and seem pretty normal from the outside, and then other days it feels like I’m just moving through the day with this weight in the background. But I’d have to really think about whether there was a clean two-week period where it was that constant.

So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I’ve had depressed periods, yes, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it was nearly every day for at least two weeks.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F34.1, F43.23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what finally made me come in is my appetite. It’s been… not normal for me, for months now. Some days I just don’t eat dinner at all, not because I’m trying to diet or anything, but because nothing sounds worth the effort. Then I’ll notice my clothes fitting differently and realize I’ve lost weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’ll sort of pick at crackers or cereal, just standing in the kitchen, because the idea of cooking a real meal just for myself feels pointless. It’s not one dramatic thing, exactly, but it’s gone on long enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that, actually. I mean, I wouldn’t say I wake up every morning thinking, “I’m depressed,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more like things have felt kind of flat, or heavy, maybe, but I don’t always have a clear word for it.\n\nI’m used to being the person who helps other people talk through what they’re feeling, so when it’s me, I tend to overthink the language. “Down” could mean a lot of things. Some days I feel okay enough to get through what I need to do, and other days I just feel… worn out by everything, even small things. But I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I guess something hasn’t felt right.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still technically do some of the things I’ve always liked, but it’s more like I’m completing the task than enjoying it.\n\nLike my garden, for example. I used to really love going out there, checking what was coming up, fussing with the plants, watering slowly after work. Now I’ll stand there with the hose and just sort of… get it done. I don’t feel much from it. Sometimes I look at the weeds or the dry spots and think, “I should deal with that,” and then I just don’t start.\n\nAnd book club too — I still show up sometimes, partly because I know people expect me to and I don’t want to be rude. But I’m not excited about the book or the conversation the way I used to be. I’ll sit there and listen, maybe make a comment or two, and then I find myself watching the clock and leaving early if I can do it without making it obvious.\n\nSo yes, I guess the pleasure part is definitely less. I’m still going through the motions, but most of it feels kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: For about how long have you been feeling this flat or heavy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say since my divorce was finalized. That was about a year and seven months ago now, which feels strange to say out loud because in some ways it feels like it just happened, and in other ways it feels like I’ve been living in this gray sort of version of my life for a long time.\n\nAt first I think I told myself it was just the adjustment, you know, the practical aftermath and all the emotional cleanup after everything was legally done. I expected I’d feel off for a while. But then the months kept going by, and I didn’t really bounce back the way I thought I would. So, roughly… a year and seven months. Since then, really.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce was finalized, were you already feeling this same kind of flat or heavy mood most days?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was quite the same before everything was finalized. I mean, I had stress before then, obviously, and the whole process leading up to it was exhausting in its own way, but this particular flat, heavy feeling… I really connect that to after the divorce was actually finalized.\n\nIt’s like once it was done, and there wasn’t the next form or next conversation or next step to focus on, that’s when this settled in. So I’d put the start of it around then — about a year and seven months ago. Since the divorce was finalized, really.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and seven months, have there been any stretches as long as two months when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I wish I could say there was a couple-month stretch where I felt like myself again, but I don’t think there has been.\n\nThere have been better days here and there, or maybe a week where I’m more functional and I think, “Okay, maybe I’m coming out of this,” but then it kind of settles back into that same heaviness. Since the divorce was finalized — so, about a year and seven months ago — it’s been pretty continuous in that way. Not always exactly the same intensity, but I can’t point to two months where I felt mostly back to normal.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word hopeless exactly… I mean, there have definitely been times where I’ve felt discouraged, or like I can’t quite picture things getting lighter in a concrete way. But “hopeless” feels like a big word to me, and I don’t know if I’m there most of the time.\n\nIt’s more like… I have trouble imagining what feeling normal again would even look like, if that makes sense. But I don’t think I’m constantly thinking, “There’s no point,” or anything that clear-cut. It’s more vague and heavy than that. So… I guess I’d say not often in a dramatic way, but there are moments where I feel pretty stuck.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. Maybe not every single day in some obvious way, but it’s been there a lot.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like replaying conversations or decisions or just whole stretches of time, and I find myself thinking, “I must have failed at something really basic.” Like being a wife, or being emotionally available enough, or noticing things sooner, or fixing things before they got to that point. And I know—at least intellectually, and I would say this to a client without hesitation—that relationships are complicated and it’s almost never just one person’s fault. I know that.\n\nBut emotionally, it doesn’t always land that way. It turns into this sense that I should have been better somehow, or I missed something that a more capable person would have understood. So yes, I guess I have often felt bad about myself in that sense. Not necessarily hating myself outright, but carrying this ongoing feeling that I failed at something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yes, in a way. Not that I have a plan to hurt myself. I don’t. I want to be clear about that. I haven’t been thinking, “I’m going to do this specific thing,” or making arrangements or anything like that.\n\nBut there are times — usually when I’m really worn down emotionally — where I catch myself thinking that it would be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And even saying that out loud is hard, because part of me immediately wants to minimize it or explain it away, but… it scares me. I know if a client said that to me, I would take it seriously. I wouldn’t just brush past it.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: I don’t want to harm myself in an active way, but I do sometimes have those thoughts about not having to keep waking up into all of this. There have definitely been stretches where I felt low a lot of the time, but whether it was most of the day, nearly every day, for a full two weeks… I don’t know. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nSince the divorce, it’s been more like this heaviness that comes and goes in waves. Some days I can function and seem pretty normal from the outside, and then other days it feels like I’m just moving through the day with this weight in the background. But I’d have to really think about whether there was a clean two-week period where it was that constant.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I wouldn’t just brush past it.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: I don’t want to harm myself in an active way, but I do sometimes have those thoughts about not having to keep waking up into all of this. There have definitely been stretches where I felt low a lot of the time, but whether it was most of the day, nearly every day, for a full two weeks… I don’t know. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nSince the divorce, it’s been more like this heaviness that comes and goes in waves. Some days I can function and seem pretty normal from the outside, and then other days it feels like I’m just moving through the day with this weight in the background. But I’d have to really think about whether there was a clean two-week period where it was that constant.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. Some days I just don’t eat dinner at all, not because I’m trying to diet or anything, but because nothing sounds worth the effort. Then I’ll notice my clothes fitting differently and realize I’ve lost weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’ll sort of pick at crackers or cereal, just standing in the kitchen, because the idea of cooking a real meal just for myself feels pointless. It’s not one dramatic thing, exactly, but it’s gone on long enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that, actually. I mean, I wouldn’t say I wake up every morning thinking, “I’m depressed,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more like things have felt kind of flat, or heavy, maybe, but I don’t always have a clear word for it.\n\nI’m used to being the person who helps other people talk through what they’re feeling, so when it’s me, I tend to overthink the language. “Down” could mean a lot of things. Some days I feel okay enough to get through what I need to do, and other days I just feel… worn out by everything, even small things. But I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I guess something hasn’t felt right.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still technically do some of the things I’ve always liked, but it’s more like I’m completing the task than enjoying it.\n\nLike my garden, for example. I used to really love going out there, checking what was coming up, fussing with the plants, watering slowly after work. Now I’ll stand there with the hose and just sort of… get it done. I don’t feel much from it. Sometimes I look at the weeds or the dry spots and think, “I should deal with that,” and then I just don’t start.\n\nAnd book club too — I still show up sometimes, partly because I know people expect me to and I don’t want to be rude. But I’m not excited about the book or the conversation the way I used to be. I’ll sit there and listen, maybe make a comment or two, and then I find myself watching the clock and leaving early if I can do it without making it obvious.\n\nSo yes, I guess the pleasure part is definitely less. I’m still going through the motions, but most of it feels kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: For about how long have you been feeling this flat or heavy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say since my divorce was finalized. I expected I’d feel off for a while. But then the months kept going by, and I didn’t really bounce back the way I thought I would. So, roughly… a year and seven months. So I’d put the start of it around then — about a year and seven months ago. I wish I could say there was a couple-month stretch where I felt like myself again, but I don’t think there has been.\n\nThere have been better days here and there, or maybe a week where I’m more functional and I think, “Okay, maybe I’m coming out of this,” but then it kind of settles back into that same heaviness. Since then, really.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce was finalized, were you already feeling this same kind of flat or heavy mood most days?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was quite the same before everything was finalized. I mean, I had stress before then, obviously, and the whole process leading up to it was exhausting in its own way, but this particular flat, heavy feeling… I really connect that to after the divorce was actually finalized.\n\nIt’s like once it was done, and there wasn’t the next form or next conversation or next step to focus on, that’s when this settled in. So I’d put the start of it around then — about a year and seven months ago. Since the divorce was finalized, really.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and seven months, have there been any stretches as long as two months when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I wish I could say there was a couple-month stretch where I felt like myself again, but I don’t think there has been.\n\nThere have been better days here and there, or maybe a week where I’m more functional and I think, “Okay, maybe I’m coming out of this,” but then it kind of settles back into that same heaviness. Since the divorce was finalized — so, about a year and seven months ago — it’s been pretty continuous in that way. Not always exactly the same intensity, but I can’t point to two months where I felt mostly back to normal.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word hopeless exactly… I mean, there have definitely been times where I’ve felt discouraged, or like I can’t quite picture things getting lighter in a concrete way. But “hopeless” feels like a big word to me, and I don’t know if I’m there most of the time.\n\nIt’s more like… I have trouble imagining what feeling normal again would even look like, if that makes sense. But I don’t think I’m constantly thinking, “There’s no point,” or anything that clear-cut. It’s more vague and heavy than that. So… I guess I’d say not often in a dramatic way, but there are moments where I feel pretty stuck.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. Maybe not every single day in some obvious way, but it’s been there a lot.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like replaying conversations or decisions or just whole stretches of time, and I find myself thinking, “I must have failed at something really basic.” Like being a wife, or being emotionally available enough, or noticing things sooner, or fixing things before they got to that point. And I know—at least intellectually, and I would say this to a client without hesitation—that relationships are complicated and it’s almost never just one person’s fault. I know that.\n\nBut emotionally, it doesn’t always land that way. It turns into this sense that I should have been better somehow, or I missed something that a more capable person would have understood. So yes, I guess I have often felt bad about myself in that sense. Not necessarily hating myself outright, but carrying this ongoing feeling that I failed at something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yes, in a way. Not that I have a plan to hurt myself. I don’t. I want to be clear about that. I haven’t been thinking, “I’m going to do this specific thing,” or making arrangements or anything like that.\n\nBut there are times — usually when I’m really worn down emotionally — where I catch myself thinking that it would be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And even saying that out loud is hard, because part of me immediately wants to minimize it or explain it away, but… it scares me. I know if a client said that to me, I would take it seriously. I wouldn’t just brush past it.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: I don’t want to harm myself in an active way, but I do sometimes have those thoughts about not having to keep waking up into all of this. And that feels frightening to admit.\n\nDoctor: Since the divorce, has there been a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because the days kind of blur together when I think back on it. There have definitely been stretches where I felt low a lot of the time, but whether it was most of the day, nearly every day, for a full two weeks… I don’t know. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nSince the divorce, it’s been more like this heaviness that comes and goes in waves. Some days I can function and seem pretty normal from the outside, and then other days it feels like I’m just moving through the day with this weight in the background. But I’d have to really think about whether there was a clean two-week period where it was that constant.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I’ve had depressed periods, yes, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it was nearly every day for at least two weeks.\n\nDoctor: After the divorce was finalized, did these symptoms continue for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely more than six months.\n\nThe divorce was finalized about a year and seven months ago, and this has really been going on since then. It hasn’t been exactly the same intensity the whole time, and I think that’s part of why I sometimes second-guess myself when I try to describe it, because there are better days or stretches where I’m functioning. But if you’re asking whether it continued past six months after it was finalized, then yes. It’s been much longer than that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, I have. It’s not usually that I can’t get into bed — I mean, I’m tired enough, and I’ll often make myself go to bed at a fairly normal time. But then I wake up really early, usually around four in the morning, and my mind just sort of… starts.

So… I guess I’d say not often in a dramatic way, but there are moments where I feel pretty stuck.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. Maybe not every single day in some obvious way, but it’s been there a lot.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like replaying conversations or decisions or just whole stretches of time, and I find myself thinking, “I must have failed at something really basic.” Like being a wife, or being emotionally available enough, or noticing things sooner, or fixing things before they got to that point. And I know—at least intellectually, and I would say this to a client without hesitation—that relationships are complicated and it’s almost never just one person’s fault. I know that.\n\nBut emotionally, it doesn’t always land that way. It turns into this sense that I should have been better somehow, or I missed something that a more capable person would have understood. So yes, I guess I have often felt bad about myself in that sense. Not necessarily hating myself outright, but carrying this ongoing feeling that I failed at something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yes, in a way. Not that I have a plan to hurt myself. I don’t. I want to be clear about that. I haven’t been thinking, “I’m going to do this specific thing,” or making arrangements or anything like that.\n\nBut there are times — usually when I’m really worn down emotionally — where I catch myself thinking that it would be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. But I don’t think I’m constantly thinking, “There’s no point,” or anything that clear-cut. It’s more vague and heavy than that. So… I guess I’d say not often in a dramatic way, but there are moments where I feel pretty stuck.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. Maybe not every single day in some obvious way, but it’s been there a lot.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like replaying conversations or decisions or just whole stretches of time, and I find myself thinking, “I must have failed at something really basic.” Like being a wife, or being emotionally available enough, or noticing things sooner, or fixing things before they got to that point. And I know—at least intellectually, and I would say this to a client without hesitation—that relationships are complicated and it’s almost never just one person’s fault. I know that.\n\nBut emotionally, it doesn’t always land that way. It turns into this sense that I should have been better somehow, or I missed something that a more capable person would have understood. So yes, I guess I have often felt bad about myself in that sense. Not necessarily hating myself outright, but carrying this ongoing feeling that I failed at something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yes, in a way. Not that I have a plan to hurt myself. I don’t. I want to be clear about that. I haven’t been thinking, “I’m going to do this specific thing,” or making arrangements or anything like that.\n\nBut there are times — usually when I’m really worn down emotionally — where I catch myself thinking that it would be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And even saying that out loud is hard, because part of me immediately wants to minimize it or explain it away, but… it scares me. I know if a client said that to me, I would take it seriously. I wouldn’t just brush past it.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: I don’t want to harm myself in an active way, but I do sometimes have those thoughts about not having to keep waking up into all of this. And that feels frightening to admit.\n\nDoctor: Since the divorce, has there been a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because the days kind of blur together when I think back on it. There have definitely been stretches where I felt low a lot of the time, but whether it was most of the day, nearly every day, for a full two weeks… I don’t know. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nSince the divorce, it’s been more like this heaviness that comes and goes in waves. Some days I can function and seem pretty normal from the outside, and then other days it feels like I’m just moving through the day with this weight in the background. But I’d have to really think about whether there was a clean two-week period where it was that constant.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I’ve had depressed periods, yes, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it was nearly every day for at least two weeks.\n\nDoctor: After the divorce was finalized, did these symptoms continue for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely more than six months.\n\nThe divorce was finalized about a year and seven months ago, and this has really been going on since then. It hasn’t been exactly the same intensity the whole time, and I think that’s part of why I sometimes second-guess myself when I try to describe it, because there are better days or stretches where I’m functioning. But if you’re asking whether it continued past six months after it was finalized, then yes. It’s been much longer than that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It’s not usually that I can’t get into bed — I mean, I’m tired enough, and I’ll often make myself go to bed at a fairly normal time. But then I wake up really early, usually around four in the morning, and my mind just sort of… starts.\n\nIt’ll be bills, or things I didn’t finish for work, or notes I need to catch up on, or something about the divorce that I thought I had already turned over a hundred times. And then I just lie there. I’m exhausted, but I can’t really fall back asleep, and I end up watching the time get closer and closer to when the alarm is going to go off.\n\nSo yes, sleep has definitely been a problem, especially that early-morning waking.\n\nDoctor: Since the divorce, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when the depression was clearly worse than your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: I guess the clearest way I can put it is that it’s been going on since the divorce was finalized, which was about a year and seven months ago.\n\nWhether there was a neat, clearly worse two-week period compared with my “usual” level… I have a harder time separating it out that cleanly. It’s felt more like this long stretch since then, with some ups and downs, rather than one obvious block of time that stands out in my mind. But the overall thing has been there for that whole period, since the divorce was final.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes. That’s actually been one of the more constant things, I think. Even on days when my caseload isn’t especially heavy — like, not one of those crisis-after-crisis days — I still feel like I’m sort of dragging my body through the hospital.

I can get through what I have to get through, most of the time, but it feels like everything takes more effort than it should. And then when I get home, I’m just… done. Too tired to cook anything real, too tired to stretch even though I know it would probably help, too tired to answer personal messages. I’ll see texts from people and think, “I should respond,” and then it feels like one more task I don’t have the energy for.

So yes, I’d say I often feel low on energy. Not every single minute, but enough that it’s become part of the way my days feel now.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what finally made me come in is my appetite. It’s been… not normal for me, for months now. Some days I just don’t eat dinner at all, not because I’m trying to diet or anything, but because nothing sounds worth the effort. Then I’ll notice my clothes fitting differently and realize I’ve lost weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’ll sort of pick at crackers or cereal, just standing in the kitchen, because the idea of cooking a real meal just for myself feels pointless. It’s not one dramatic thing, exactly, but it’s gone on long enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that, actually. I mean, I wouldn’t say I wake up every morning thinking, “I’m depressed,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more like things have felt kind of flat, or heavy, maybe, but I don’t always have a clear word for it.\n\nI’m used to being the person who helps other people talk through what they’re feeling, so when it’s me, I tend to overthink the language. “Down” could mean a lot of things. Some days I feel okay enough to get through what I need to do, and other days I just feel… worn out by everything, even small things. But I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I still technically do some of the things I’ve always liked, but it’s more like I’m completing the task than enjoying it.\n\nLike my garden, for example. I used to really love going out there, checking what was coming up, fussing with the plants, watering slowly after work. Now I’ll stand there with the hose and just sort of… get it done. I don’t feel much from it. Sometimes I look at the weeds or the dry spots and think, “I should deal with that,” and then I just don’t start.\n\nAnd book club too — I still show up sometimes, partly because I know people expect me to and I don’t want to be rude. But I’m not excited about the book or the conversation the way I used to be. I’ll sit there and listen, maybe make a comment or two, and then I find myself watching the clock and leaving early if I can do it without making it obvious.\n\nSo yes, I guess the pleasure part is definitely less. I’m still going through the motions, but most of it feels kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: For about how long have you been feeling this flat or heavy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say since my divorce was finalized. That was about a year and seven months ago now, which feels strange to say out loud because in some ways it feels like it just happened, and in other ways it feels like I’ve been living in this gray sort of version of my life for a long time.\n\nAt first I think I told myself it was just the adjustment, you know, the practical aftermath and all the emotional cleanup after everything was legally done. I expected I’d feel off for a while. But then the months kept going by, and I didn’t really bounce back the way I thought I would. So, roughly… a year and seven months. Since then, really.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce was finalized, were you already feeling this same kind of flat or heavy mood most days?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was quite the same before everything was finalized. I mean, I had stress before then, obviously, and the whole process leading up to it was exhausting in its own way, but this particular flat, heavy feeling… I really connect that to after the divorce was actually finalized.\n\nIt’s like once it was done, and there wasn’t the next form or next conversation or next step to focus on, that’s when this settled in. So I’d put the start of it around then — about a year and seven months ago. Since the divorce was finalized, really.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and seven months, have there been any stretches as long as two months when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I wish I could say there was a couple-month stretch where I felt like myself again, but I don’t think there has been.\n\nThere have been better days here and there, or maybe a week where I’m more functional and I think, “Okay, maybe I’m coming out of this,” but then it kind of settles back into that same heaviness. Since the divorce was finalized — so, about a year and seven months ago — it’s been pretty continuous in that way. Not always exactly the same intensity, but I can’t point to two months where I felt mostly back to normal.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word hopeless exactly… I mean, there have definitely been times where I’ve felt discouraged, or like I can’t quite picture things getting lighter in a concrete way. But “hopeless” feels like a big word to me, and I don’t know if I’m there most of the time.\n\nIt’s more like… I have trouble imagining what feeling normal again would even look like, if that makes sense. But I don’t think I’m constantly thinking, “There’s no point,” or anything that clear-cut. It’s more vague and heavy than that. So… I guess I’d say not often in a dramatic way, but there are moments where I feel pretty stuck.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. Maybe not every single day in some obvious way, but it’s been there a lot.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like replaying conversations or decisions or just whole stretches of time, and I find myself thinking, “I must have failed at something really basic.” Like being a wife, or being emotionally available enough, or noticing things sooner, or fixing things before they got to that point. And I know—at least intellectually, and I would say this to a client without hesitation—that relationships are complicated and it’s almost never just one person’s fault. I know that.\n\nBut emotionally, it doesn’t always land that way. It turns into this sense that I should have been better somehow, or I missed something that a more capable person would have understood. So yes, I guess I have often felt bad about myself in that sense. Not necessarily hating myself outright, but carrying this ongoing feeling that I failed at something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yes, in a way. Not that I have a plan to hurt myself. I don’t. I want to be clear about that. I haven’t been thinking, “I’m going to do this specific thing,” or making arrangements or anything like that.\n\nBut there are times — usually when I’m really worn down emotionally — where I catch myself thinking that it would be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And even saying that out loud is hard, because part of me immediately wants to minimize it or explain it away, but… it scares me. I know if a client said that to me, I would take it seriously. I wouldn’t just brush past it.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: I don’t want to harm myself in an active way, but I do sometimes have those thoughts about not having to keep waking up into all of this. And that feels frightening to admit.\n\nDoctor: Since the divorce, has there been a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because the days kind of blur together when I think back on it. There have definitely been stretches where I felt low a lot of the time, but whether it was most of the day, nearly every day, for a full two weeks… I don’t know. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nSince the divorce, it’s been more like this heaviness that comes and goes in waves. Some days I can function and seem pretty normal from the outside, and then other days it feels like I’m just moving through the day with this weight in the background. But I’d have to really think about whether there was a clean two-week period where it was that constant.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I’ve had depressed periods, yes, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it was nearly every day for at least two weeks.\n\nDoctor: After the divorce was finalized, did these symptoms continue for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely more than six months.\n\nThe divorce was finalized about a year and seven months ago, and this has really been going on since then. It hasn’t been exactly the same intensity the whole time, and I think that’s part of why I sometimes second-guess myself when I try to describe it, because there are better days or stretches where I’m functioning. But if you’re asking whether it continued past six months after it was finalized, then yes. It’s been much longer than that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It’s not usually that I can’t get into bed — I mean, I’m tired enough, and I’ll often make myself go to bed at a fairly normal time. But then I wake up really early, usually around four in the morning, and my mind just sort of… starts.\n\nIt’ll be bills, or things I didn’t finish for work, or notes I need to catch up on, or something about the divorce that I thought I had already turned over a hundred times. And then I just lie there. I’m exhausted, but I can’t really fall back asleep, and I end up watching the time get closer and closer to when the alarm is going to go off.\n\nSo yes, sleep has definitely been a problem, especially that early-morning waking.\n\nDoctor: Since the divorce, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when the depression was clearly worse than your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: I guess the clearest way I can put it is that it’s been going on since the divorce was finalized, which was about a year and seven months ago.\n\nWhether there was a neat, clearly worse two-week period compared with my “usual” level… I have a harder time separating it out that cleanly. It’s felt more like this long stretch since then, with some ups and downs, rather than one obvious block of time that stands out in my mind. But the overall thing has been there for that whole period, since the divorce was final.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt low on energy or fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually been one of the more constant things, I think. Even on days when my caseload isn’t especially heavy — like, not one of those crisis-after-crisis days — I still feel like I’m sort of dragging my body through the hospital.\n\nI can get through what I have to get through, most of the time, but it feels like everything takes more effort than it should. Not every single minute, but enough that it’s become part of the way my days feel now.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring this time, have you often had trouble concentrating or making decisions?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",

{"key":"S031","name":"Diminished_Thinking","description":"Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.","reason":"The doctor is asking about trouble concentrating or making decisions, which directly matches diminished thinking and indecisiveness."}

But then I wake up really early, usually around four in the morning, and my mind just sort of… starts.\n\nIt’ll be bills, or things I didn’t finish for work, or notes I need to catch up on, or something about the divorce that I thought I had already turned over a hundred times. And then I just lie there. I’m exhausted, but I can’t really fall back asleep, and I end up watching the time get closer and closer to when the alarm is going to go off.\n\nSo yes, sleep has definitely been a problem, especially that early-morning waking.\n\nDoctor: Since the divorce, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when the depression was clearly worse than your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: I guess the clearest way I can put it is that it’s been going on since the divorce was finalized, which was about a year and seven months ago.\n\nWhether there was a neat, clearly worse two-week period compared with my “usual” level… I have a harder time separating it out that cleanly. It’s felt more like this long stretch since then, with some ups and downs, rather than one obvious block of time that stands out in my mind. But the overall thing has been there for that whole period, since the divorce was final.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt low on energy or fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I have noticed that. At work especially, during case reviews, I’ll be reading through notes that I know are straightforward — like updates I should be able to absorb pretty quickly — and I’ll realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph two or three times and still haven’t really taken it in. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because this is work I’m used to doing, and I’ve always relied on being fairly organized and attentive.

And decisions… even small ones can feel weirdly hard. Like deciding which client call to return first, when normally I’d just prioritize and move through the list. Now I can sit there staring at the names, going back and forth, second-guessing myself. Even something as ordinary as what to buy for dinner can take me a ridiculous amount of time, like I’m standing in the store unable to make a simple choice.

It’s not that I can’t think at all, but everything feels slower and more effortful than it used to.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF34.1, F32.9, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what finally made me come in is my appetite. It’s been… not normal for me, for months now. Some days I just don’t eat dinner at all, not because I’m trying to diet or anything, but because nothing sounds worth the effort. Then I’ll notice my clothes fitting differently and realize I’ve lost weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’ll sort of pick at crackers or cereal, just standing in the kitchen, because the idea of cooking a real meal just for myself feels pointless. It’s not one dramatic thing, exactly, but it’s gone on long enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that, actually. I mean, I wouldn’t say I wake up every morning thinking, “I’m depressed,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more like things have felt kind of flat, or heavy, maybe, but I don’t always have a clear word for it.\n\nI’m used to being the person who helps other people talk through what they’re feeling, so when it’s me, I tend to overthink the language. “Down” could mean a lot of things. Some days I feel okay enough to get through what I need to do, and other days I just feel… worn out by everything, even small things. But I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I guess something hasn’t felt right.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still technically do some of the things I’ve always liked, but it’s more like I’m completing the task than enjoying it.\n\nLike my garden, for example. I used to really love going out there, checking what was coming up, fussing with the plants, watering slowly after work. Now I’ll stand there with the hose and just sort of… get it done. I don’t feel much from it. Sometimes I look at the weeds or the dry spots and think, “I should deal with that,” and then I just don’t start.\n\nAnd book club too — I still show up sometimes, partly because I know people expect me to and I don’t want to be rude. But I’m not excited about the book or the conversation the way I used to be. I’ll sit there and listen, maybe make a comment or two, and then I find myself watching the clock and leaving early if I can do it without making it obvious.\n\nSo yes, I guess the pleasure part is definitely less. I’m still going through the motions, but most of it feels kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: For about how long have you been feeling this flat or heavy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say since my divorce was finalized. That was about a year and seven months ago now, which feels strange to say out loud because in some ways it feels like it just happened, and in other ways it feels like I’ve been living in this gray sort of version of my life for a long time.\n\nAt first I think I told myself it was just the adjustment, you know, the practical aftermath and all the emotional cleanup after everything was legally done. I expected I’d feel off for a while. But then the months kept going by, and I didn’t really bounce back the way I thought I would. So, roughly… a year and seven months. Since then, really.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce was finalized, were you already feeling this same kind of flat or heavy mood most days?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was quite the same before everything was finalized. I mean, I had stress before then, obviously, and the whole process leading up to it was exhausting in its own way, but this particular flat, heavy feeling… I really connect that to after the divorce was actually finalized.\n\nIt’s like once it was done, and there wasn’t the next form or next conversation or next step to focus on, that’s when this settled in. So I’d put the start of it around then — about a year and seven months ago. Since the divorce was finalized, really.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and seven months, have there been any stretches as long as two months when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I wish I could say there was a couple-month stretch where I felt like myself again, but I don’t think there has been.\n\nThere have been better days here and there, or maybe a week where I’m more functional and I think, “Okay, maybe I’m coming out of this,” but then it kind of settles back into that same heaviness. Since the divorce was finalized — so, about a year and seven months ago — it’s been pretty continuous in that way. Not always exactly the same intensity, but I can’t point to two months where I felt mostly back to normal.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word hopeless exactly… I mean, there have definitely been times where I’ve felt discouraged, or like I can’t quite picture things getting lighter in a concrete way. But “hopeless” feels like a big word to me, and I don’t know if I’m there most of the time.\n\nIt’s more like… I have trouble imagining what feeling normal again would even look like, if that makes sense. But I don’t think I’m constantly thinking, “There’s no point,” or anything that clear-cut. It’s more vague and heavy than that. So… I guess I’d say not often in a dramatic way, but there are moments where I feel pretty stuck.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. Maybe not every single day in some obvious way, but it’s been there a lot.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like replaying conversations or decisions or just whole stretches of time, and I find myself thinking, “I must have failed at something really basic.” Like being a wife, or being emotionally available enough, or noticing things sooner, or fixing things before they got to that point. And I know—at least intellectually, and I would say this to a client without hesitation—that relationships are complicated and it’s almost never just one person’s fault. I know that.\n\nBut emotionally, it doesn’t always land that way. It turns into this sense that I should have been better somehow, or I missed something that a more capable person would have understood. So yes, I guess I have often felt bad about myself in that sense. Not necessarily hating myself outright, but carrying this ongoing feeling that I failed at something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yes, in a way. Not that I have a plan to hurt myself. I don’t. I want to be clear about that. I haven’t been thinking, “I’m going to do this specific thing,” or making arrangements or anything like that.\n\nBut there are times — usually when I’m really worn down emotionally — where I catch myself thinking that it would be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And even saying that out loud is hard, because part of me immediately wants to minimize it or explain it away, but… it scares me. I know if a client said that to me, I would take it seriously. I wouldn’t just brush past it.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: I don’t want to harm myself in an active way, but I do sometimes have those thoughts about not having to keep waking up into all of this. And that feels frightening to admit.\n\nDoctor: Since the divorce, has there been a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because the days kind of blur together when I think back on it. There have definitely been stretches where I felt low a lot of the time, but whether it was most of the day, nearly every day, for a full two weeks… I don’t know. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nSince the divorce, it’s been more like this heaviness that comes and goes in waves. Some days I can function and seem pretty normal from the outside, and then other days it feels like I’m just moving through the day with this weight in the background. But I’d have to really think about whether there was a clean two-week period where it was that constant.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I’ve had depressed periods, yes, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it was nearly every day for at least two weeks.\n\nDoctor: After the divorce was finalized, did these symptoms continue for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely more than six months.\n\nThe divorce was finalized about a year and seven months ago, and this has really been going on since then. It hasn’t been exactly the same intensity the whole time, and I think that’s part of why I sometimes second-guess myself when I try to describe it, because there are better days or stretches where I’m functioning. But if you’re asking whether it continued past six months after it was finalized, then yes. It’s been much longer than that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It’s not usually that I can’t get into bed — I mean, I’m tired enough, and I’ll often make myself go to bed at a fairly normal time. But then I wake up really early, usually around four in the morning, and my mind just sort of… starts.\n\nIt’ll be bills, or things I didn’t finish for work, or notes I need to catch up on, or something about the divorce that I thought I had already turned over a hundred times. And then I just lie there. I’m exhausted, but I can’t really fall back asleep, and I end up watching the time get closer and closer to when the alarm is going to go off.\n\nSo yes, sleep has definitely been a problem, especially that early-morning waking.\n\nDoctor: Since the divorce, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when the depression was clearly worse than your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: I guess the clearest way I can put it is that it’s been going on since the divorce was finalized, which was about a year and seven months ago.\n\nWhether there was a neat, clearly worse two-week period compared with my “usual” level… I have a harder time separating it out that cleanly. It’s felt more like this long stretch since then, with some ups and downs, rather than one obvious block of time that stands out in my mind. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I think… yes, probably. I hesitate a little because “most of the day” sounds so exact, and I don’t know that I’ve been tracking it in that kind of way, hour by hour. But if I’m being honest, more days than not, there’s been this low feeling running underneath everything.

Some days I just don’t eat dinner at all, not because I’m trying to diet or anything, but because nothing sounds worth the effort. Then I’ll notice my clothes fitting differently and realize I’ve lost weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’ll sort of pick at crackers or cereal, just standing in the kitchen, because the idea of cooking a real meal just for myself feels pointless. It’s not one dramatic thing, exactly, but it’s gone on long enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that, actually. I mean, I wouldn’t say I wake up every morning thinking, “I’m depressed,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more like things have felt kind of flat, or heavy, maybe, but I don’t always have a clear word for it.\n\nI’m used to being the person who helps other people talk through what they’re feeling, so when it’s me, I tend to overthink the language. “Down” could mean a lot of things. Some days I feel okay enough to get through what I need to do, and other days I just feel… worn out by everything, even small things. But I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I guess something hasn’t felt right.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still technically do some of the things I’ve always liked, but it’s more like I’m completing the task than enjoying it.\n\nLike my garden, for example. I used to really love going out there, checking what was coming up, fussing with the plants, watering slowly after work. Now I’ll stand there with the hose and just sort of… get it done. I don’t feel much from it. Sometimes I look at the weeds or the dry spots and think, “I should deal with that,” and then I just don’t start.\n\nAnd book club too — I still show up sometimes, partly because I know people expect me to and I don’t want to be rude. But I’m not excited about the book or the conversation the way I used to be. I’ll sit there and listen, maybe make a comment or two, and then I find myself watching the clock and leaving early if I can do it without making it obvious.\n\nSo yes, I guess the pleasure part is definitely less. I’m still going through the motions, but most of it feels kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: For about how long have you been feeling this flat or heavy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say since my divorce was finalized. That was about a year and seven months ago now, which feels strange to say out loud because in some ways it feels like it just happened, and in other ways it feels like I’ve been living in this gray sort of version of my life for a long time.\n\nAt first I think I told myself it was just the adjustment, you know, the practical aftermath and all the emotional cleanup after everything was legally done. I expected I’d feel off for a while. But then the months kept going by, and I didn’t really bounce back the way I thought I would. So, roughly… a year and seven months. Since then, really.\n\nDoctor: Before the divorce was finalized, were you already feeling this same kind of flat or heavy mood most days?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was quite the same before everything was finalized. I mean, I had stress before then, obviously, and the whole process leading up to it was exhausting in its own way, but this particular flat, heavy feeling… I really connect that to after the divorce was actually finalized.\n\nIt’s like once it was done, and there wasn’t the next form or next conversation or next step to focus on, that’s when this settled in. So I’d put the start of it around then — about a year and seven months ago. Since the divorce was finalized, really.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and seven months, have there been any stretches as long as two months when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I wish I could say there was a couple-month stretch where I felt like myself again, but I don’t think there has been.\n\nThere have been better days here and there, or maybe a week where I’m more functional and I think, “Okay, maybe I’m coming out of this,” but then it kind of settles back into that same heaviness. Since the divorce was finalized — so, about a year and seven months ago — it’s been pretty continuous in that way. Not always exactly the same intensity, but I can’t point to two months where I felt mostly back to normal.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word hopeless exactly… I mean, there have definitely been times where I’ve felt discouraged, or like I can’t quite picture things getting lighter in a concrete way. But “hopeless” feels like a big word to me, and I don’t know if I’m there most of the time.\n\nIt’s more like… I have trouble imagining what feeling normal again would even look like, if that makes sense. But I don’t think I’m constantly thinking, “There’s no point,” or anything that clear-cut. It’s more vague and heavy than that. So… I guess I’d say not often in a dramatic way, but there are moments where I feel pretty stuck.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. Maybe not every single day in some obvious way, but it’s been there a lot.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, like replaying conversations or decisions or just whole stretches of time, and I find myself thinking, “I must have failed at something really basic.” Like being a wife, or being emotionally available enough, or noticing things sooner, or fixing things before they got to that point. And I know—at least intellectually, and I would say this to a client without hesitation—that relationships are complicated and it’s almost never just one person’s fault. I know that.\n\nBut emotionally, it doesn’t always land that way. It turns into this sense that I should have been better somehow, or I missed something that a more capable person would have understood. So yes, I guess I have often felt bad about myself in that sense. Not necessarily hating myself outright, but carrying this ongoing feeling that I failed at something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yes, in a way. Not that I have a plan to hurt myself. I don’t. I want to be clear about that. I haven’t been thinking, “I’m going to do this specific thing,” or making arrangements or anything like that.\n\nBut there are times — usually when I’m really worn down emotionally — where I catch myself thinking that it would be a relief if I just didn’t wake up. And even saying that out loud is hard, because part of me immediately wants to minimize it or explain it away, but… it scares me. I know if a client said that to me, I would take it seriously. I wouldn’t just brush past it.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: I don’t want to harm myself in an active way, but I do sometimes have those thoughts about not having to keep waking up into all of this. And that feels frightening to admit.\n\nDoctor: Since the divorce, has there been a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because the days kind of blur together when I think back on it. There have definitely been stretches where I felt low a lot of the time, but whether it was most of the day, nearly every day, for a full two weeks… I don’t know. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nSince the divorce, it’s been more like this heaviness that comes and goes in waves. Some days I can function and seem pretty normal from the outside, and then other days it feels like I’m just moving through the day with this weight in the background. But I’d have to really think about whether there was a clean two-week period where it was that constant.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I’ve had depressed periods, yes, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it was nearly every day for at least two weeks.\n\nDoctor: After the divorce was finalized, did these symptoms continue for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely more than six months.\n\nThe divorce was finalized about a year and seven months ago, and this has really been going on since then. It hasn’t been exactly the same intensity the whole time, and I think that’s part of why I sometimes second-guess myself when I try to describe it, because there are better days or stretches where I’m functioning. But if you’re asking whether it continued past six months after it was finalized, then yes. It’s been much longer than that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It’s not usually that I can’t get into bed — I mean, I’m tired enough, and I’ll often make myself go to bed at a fairly normal time. But then I wake up really early, usually around four in the morning, and my mind just sort of… starts.\n\nIt’ll be bills, or things I didn’t finish for work, or notes I need to catch up on, or something about the divorce that I thought I had already turned over a hundred times. And then I just lie there. I’m exhausted, but I can’t really fall back asleep, and I end up watching the time get closer and closer to when the alarm is going to go off.\n\nSo yes, sleep has definitely been a problem, especially that early-morning waking.\n\nDoctor: Since the divorce, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when the depression was clearly worse than your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: I guess the clearest way I can put it is that it’s been going on since the divorce was finalized, which was about a year and seven months ago.\n\nWhether there was a neat, clearly worse two-week period compared with my “usual” level… I have a harder time separating it out that cleanly. It’s felt more like this long stretch since then, with some ups and downs, rather than one obvious block of time that stands out in my mind. But the overall thing has been there for that whole period, since the divorce was final.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often felt low on energy or fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually been one of the more constant things, I think. Even on days when my caseload isn’t especially heavy — like, not one of those crisis-after-crisis days — I still feel like I’m sort of dragging my body through the hospital.\n\nI can get through what I have to get through, most of the time, but it feels like everything takes more effort than it should. And then when I get home, I’m just… done. Too tired to cook anything real, too tired to stretch even though I know it would probably help, too tired to answer personal messages. I’ll see texts from people and think, “I should respond,” and then it feels like one more task I don’t have the energy for.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I often feel low on energy. Not every single minute, but enough that it’s become part of the way my days feel now.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you often had trouble concentrating or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have noticed that. At work especially, during case reviews, I’ll be reading through notes that I know are straightforward — like updates I should be able to absorb pretty quickly — and I’ll realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph two or three times and still haven’t really taken it in. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because this is work I’m used to doing, and I’ve always relied on being fairly organized and attentive.\n\nAnd decisions… even small ones can feel weirdly hard. Like deciding which client call to return first, when normally I’d just prioritize and move through the list. Now I can sit there staring at the names, going back and forth, second-guessing myself. Even something as ordinary as what to buy for dinner can take me a ridiculous amount of time, like I’m standing in the store unable to make a simple choice.\n\nIt’s not that I can’t think at all, but everything feels slower and more effortful than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Since the divorce, was there ever a time when these symptoms became much more severe than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a separate time when it became much more severe than usual. It’s more that this whole thing has been going on since the divorce was finalized — about a year and seven months ago now — and it’s kind of settled in as this ongoing level of feeling unwell.\n\nThere have been better and worse days, definitely, but when you ask whether there was a particular period where it got sharply worse, I have trouble separating it that way. In my mind, it’s tied to the divorce being finalized, and since then it’s just… been there.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and seven months, have you felt depressed most of the day on more days than not?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I hesitate a little because “most of the day” sounds so exact, and I don’t know that I’ve been tracking it in that kind of way, hour by hour. But if I’m being honest, more days than not, there’s been this low feeling running underneath everything.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, like I’m crying all day or completely unable to function. A lot of the time I’m still going through the motions, doing what needs to be done, talking to people, getting through the day. But underneath that, I feel sad or weighed down for a large part of the day. Sometimes it’s worse in the mornings, sometimes it sort of creeps in later, but it’s there often enough that I don’t think I can say it’s occasional.\n\nSo… yes, over this past year and seven months, I’d say I have felt depressed most of the day on more days than not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates":["F34.1","F32.9"],"note":"Sustained depressed mood most of the day on more days than not for >2 years threshold is not met, but duration >1 year 7 months with low energy, poor concentration, low self-worth, appetite/weight loss, insomnia, and passive death wishes strongly supports persistent depressive disorder pattern; adjustment disorder is ruled out because symptoms persisted well beyond 6 months after the stressor and are better accounted for by a depressive disorder.","is_final":false}

---------- turn 18 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------
========== INPUT [patient] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Major Depressive Disorder.
